ABBREVIATIONS. 

I.,     Indiction ;    1.1.,    longest   liver,    survivor;    q.,    quondam,    i.e.,

PREFACE. 


The  Protocol  Book  of  Cuthbert  Simson,  published  by  the  Grampian  Club 
twenty  years  ago,  has  to  some  extent  familiarised  students  of  local  history 
with  the  general  characteristics  of  the  class  of  records  to  which  it  belongs. 
But  while  all  the  protocol  books  of  notaries  officiating  in  this  country 
possess  features  in  common,  their  distinctive  peculiarities  vary  with  the  sphere 
in  which  the  notary  exercised  his  vocation.  Simson  was  chapter  clerk  of 
Glasgow  at  a  time  when  the  ancient  church  was  powerful  in  the  land.  He 
had  a  large  share,  if  not  a  monopoly,  of  the  notarial  business  which  the 
Glasgow  clerics  had  at  their  disposal,  and,  accordingly,  in  his  register 
are  to  be  found  not  only  particulars  regarding  properties  throughout  the 
diocese,  but  also  accounts  of  proceedings  against  heretics  and  other  trans- 
gressors of  ecclesiastical  law,  together  with  references  to  the  various  matters 
coming  under  the  wide  jurisdiction  of  the  consistorial  court.  Contrasted  with 
this  extensive  range  of  subjects,  the  Protocols  now  introduced  to  the  notice 
of  the  public  deal  with  matters  comprehended  within  narrow  limits,  being 
confined  almost  exclusively  to  transfers  of  property  in  Glasgow  and  the 
western  shires;  but  still  the  stores  of  topographical  and  genealogical  infor- 
mation which  they  contain  are  such  that  their  publication  will  doubtless  be 
welcomed   by   investigators   into   these    branches   of   knowledge. 

Outside  legal  and  archaeological  circles  so  little  is  known  concerning 
Notaries  and  their  Protocols  that  it  has  been  thought  desirable  to  give 
a   brief   outline   of   their   history. 

Under  the  Roman  system  of  jurisprudence,  Public  Notaries,  or  Tabellions, 
as  they  were  otherwise  designated,  were  erected  into  a  school  or  college, 
governed  by  a  superior  officer  holding  commission  from  the  Emperor,  and 
care  was  taken  that  none  should  be  admitted  into  the  body  except  those 
who  were  of  good  fame  and  skilful  in  the  laws.  The  members  were  employed 
in  preparing  agreements  and  forming  public  instruments  regarding  them, 
which  were  treated  as  probative  and  entitled  to  receive  legal  effect;  and 
they  also  formed  the  acts  and  judicial  proceedings  of  the  courts  of  law.  After 
the  fall  of  the  Roman  empire,  the  Emperors  who  succeeded  to  the  title 
borne   by   the    Caesars,    and    claimed    their    prerogatives,    were   accustomed    to 


viii  PREFACE. 

appoint  Imperial  Notaries,  who  were  authorised  to  exercise  their  office  in 
any  country  which  had  ever  been  subject  to  the  Roman  authority.  The 
Popes  also,  in  virtue  of  their  spiritual  supremacy,  asserted  universal  juris- 
diction, and  appointed  Apostolic  notaries,  for  whom  was  claimed  the  privilege 
of  exercising  their  office  in  any  part  of  the  world,  both  in  spiritual  and 
temporal  affairs,  and  to  the  exclusion  of  Imperial  Notaries  in  spiritual  con- 
cerns. For  securing  the  privileges  of  both  orders,  Notaries  frequently  procured 
admission  in  a  two-fold  capacity,  and  these  in  their  instruments  are  designed 
as  acting  "Apostolica  et  Inrperiali  auctoritatibus."  In  this  country  an  im- 
portant change  was  made  in  the  system  of  admission  by  an  act  of  the 
Scottish  Parliament,  passed  in  the  tenth  year  cf  the  reign  of  King  James 
III.  (1469  c.  6).  Proceeding  on  the  preamble  that  "oure  Soverane  Lord 
hes  ful  jurisdictioune  and  fre  impire  within  his  realme,"  it  was  ordained 
"that  his  hienes  may  mak  notaries  and  tabelliones  quhais  instrumentis  sal 
have  ful  faith  in  all  contractis  civile  within  the  realme;  and  in  tyme 
cummyn  that  na  notaris  maid,  nor  to  be  maid,  be  the  Imperouris  autorite 
have  faith  in  contractis  civile  within  the  realme,  les  than  he  be  examinyt 
be  the  ordinare  (i.e.,  the  judge  ordinary  in  the  ecclesiastical  courts),  and 
approvit  be  the  Kingis  hienes."  While  the  authority  of  the  Imperial  notaries 
was  thus  curtailed,  it  was  provided  that  "ful  faith  be  givin  to  the  Papale 
notaries  in  tymes  bygane  and  to  cum  in  all  thare  instrumentis."  With 
regard  to  the  notaries  to  be  appointed  in  future  by  the  King,  it  was 
ordained  that  they  "be  examinit  before  thair  ordinaris  bischopis,  and  have 
certificatioune  of  them  that  thai  ar  of  faith,  gude  fame,  science  and  lawte 
(loyalty),  according  to  the  said  office."  Subsequent  to  this  act,  notaries  who 
had  been  admitted  by  Imperial  authority,  and  in  compliance  with  the  new 
law  obtained  the  Royal  approval,  were  designed  as  holding  office  "auctori- 
tatibus   Imperiali   et    Regali." 

Another  act  of  parliament,  passed  in  1503  (c.  8),  alludes  to  complaints 
made  by  the  lieges  against  the  mal-practices  of  "fals  notaris,"  and  regulations 
were  set  down  for  preventing  fraud  and  insuring  greater  efficiency.  The 
ecclesiastical  judges,  "bischopis  and  ordinaris,"  were  directed  to  call  before 
them  all  the  notaries  within  their  dioceses,  to  examine  them  upon  their 
qualifications  and  demeanour,  and  those  found  acceptable  were  to  be  reported 
to  the  King,  who  was  to  depute  certain  persons  to  examine  them  farther, 
"and  gif  thai  be  ganand  (i.e.,  found  fit),  to  mak  them  regale  gif  thai  be 
nocht   maid    regale   of   befor." 


PREFACE.  ix 

Previous  to  this  time  notaries  belonged  exclusively  to  the  class  of  church- 
men, but  from  the  terms  of  a  statute  passed  in  15-10  (c.  37),  it  is  to  be  inferred 
that  laymen  were  then  beginning  to  enter  the  profession.  By  this  act  sheriffs 
were  required  to  examine  laic  notaries,  and  the  "ordinaries"  all  notaries 
spiritual,  within  their  respective  bounds,  and  to  admit  them  by  acts  of  court; 
and,  with  the  view  of  securing  uniformity  in  the  attestation  of  instruments, 
notaries  were  required  to  insert  in  a  book  the  signs  and  subscriptions  to  be 
used  by  them  in  all  instruments  passing  under  their  hands.  By  the  statute 
1551  (c.  17),  which  sets  forth  that  the  acts  concerning  notaries  had  not 
hitherto  been  put  to  due  execution,  it  was  ordered  that  the  laws  should 
be  duly  enforced,  and  (c.  19)  that  within  a  certain  time  sheriffs  should 
bring  or  send  all  "notars  temporall  men,"  and  the  ordinaries  "all  spiritual 
notars,"  within  their  respective  bounds,  to  the  burgh  of  Edinburgh,  and  there 
present  them  to  the  lords  of  council  to  be  examined,  and  (if  found  qualified) 
admitted  to  the  office,  till  which  admission  their  instruments  were  to  have 
no  effect.  It  would  seem,  however,  that  the  laws  "anent  notars"  were  not 
much  honoured  in  the  observance,  because  the  next  statute  on  the  subject 
(1555,  c.  18)  again  refers  to  the  preceding  acts  as  having  "tane  na  dew  and 
effectual  execution."  Sheriffs,  bailies,  and  others,  were  therefore  directed  to 
cause  the  notaries  within  their  respective  districts  to  appear  before  the  lords 
of  council  at  Edinburgh,  "bringand  with  them  thair  creatiounis  and  haill 
protocollis  .  .  .  to  be  examinat,  and  their  creatiounis  viseit  by  thame, 
their  protocollis  producit  to  be  markit  be  the  saidis  lordis,  and  the  levis 
numberit  and  the  blankis  markit  .  .  .  And  as  thai  be  fundin  qualifeit 
and  admittit  be  the  saidis  lordis  of  counsall  to  use  the  office  of  notar 
thairefter."  If  any  notaries  should  be  convicted  of  "falset,"  their  moveable 
property  was  ordered  to  be  forfeited,  "and  they  to  want  thair  rycht  hand,  and 
to  be  banist  the  realme  for  ever."  Commenting  on  the  dismemberment  part  of 
the  punishment,  the  author  of  a  work  on  "The  Office  of  a  Notary  Public" 
(1812)  complacently  remarks  that  "the  writing  hand  being  regularly  the  right 
one,  our  statute  orders  the  loss  of  it;  and  the  reason  of  this  punishment 
seems  to  be  grounded  upon  the  equity  of  the  thing,  for  it  is  but  reasonable 
that,  that  part   of   the   body   should   suffer   which   committed   the   crime." 

With  the  abolition  of  consistorial  jurisdictions  consequent  on  the  Reformation, 
farther  changes  in  the  system  of  admitting  notaries  were  introduced.  By 
the  Act  1563  (c.  16),  it  was  ordained  that  all  notaries  who  had  not  as 
yet  been   examined   by   the   lords   of   council   should    appear   before    them    for 


x  PREFACE. 

that  purpose,  otherwise  all  instruments  taken  by  them  should  be  void;  and 
further  (c.  17),  that  in  future  no  one  should  exercise  the  office  "without 
thay  be  maid  and  creat  be  the  Querns  Majesteis  speciall  letteris,  and  there- 
efter  examinat  and  admittet  be  the  lordis  of  sessioun  and  college  of  justice,  quha 
sail  tak  thair  aithis  .  .  .  and  caus  register  thair  signe  and  subscriptioun 
quhilk  thai  sail  use  in  all  tymes  efter  thair  said  admissioun."  By  acts  passed 
in  1567,  no  one  was  to  be  admitted  a  notary  who  did  not  profess  the 
Evangell,  and  all  were  to  be  discharged  and  none  to  be  admitted  thereafter 
except  those  who  were  of  the  religion  then  received.  By  another  act  of 
the  same  year,  which  has  worked  beneficially  for  the  preservation  of  these 
ancient  registers,  it  was  provided  (c.  87),  that  the  protocol  books  of  notaries 
within  the  shire  should  be  brought  to  the  sheriff  of  the  shire,  and  the  books 
of  those  within  burghs  to  the  provost  and  bailies  for  custody.  Subsequent 
statutes  (1587  c.  29  and  1617  c.  22)  provided  for  the  delivery  to  the  Clerk 
of  Register  in  Edinburgh  of  all  protocols  excepting  those  of  clerks  of  burghs, 
which   were   to   remain   with   the   magistrates. 

Another  act,  passed  in  1567  (c.  34),  requires  also  to  be  noticed.  It 
proceeds  on  a  narrative  of  "the  greit  hurt  done  of  befoir  within  burgh  be 
geving  of  sesingis  privatlie  without  ane  bailie"  and  "ane  commone  clerk  of 
burgh,  quairthrow  our  Soverane  Lordis  liegis  may  be  defraudit  greitlie,"  and 
enacts  that  "na  sesing  be  gevin  within  burgh  of  ony  maner  of  land  or 
tenement  within  the  samin,  in  ony  tyme  aiming,  bot  be  ane  of  the  baillics 
of  the  burgh  and  the  commoun  clerk  thairof."  From  this  time  therefore  it 
may  be  assumed  that  the  protocol  books  of  the  town  clerks  of  Glasgow 
record  all  transactions  relating  to  heritable  property  within  the  ancient 
royalty. 

Such  is  a  summary  of  the  legislation  bearing  upon  Notaries  and  their 
Protocols.  William  Hegait,  the  writer  of  the  Protocols  of  which  abstracts 
are  given  in  the  following  pages,  was  created  a  notary  by  apostolic 
authority  in  the  year  1544.  Complying  with  the  requirements  of  the  statute 
1563  (c.  16),  he  attended  before  the  lords  of  council  at  Edinburgh,  in  February, 
1563-4,  produced  the  instrument  vouching  his  "creation,"  with  his  three 
Protocol  Books  for  inspection,  and  having  been  found  duly  qualified,  was 
authorised  to  continue  in  the  exercise  of  his  office.  In  the  instruments 
passing  under  his  hand  previous  to  this  time,  Hegait's  designation  is  "Clericus 
Glasguensis  diocesis,  auctoritate  sacra  Apostolica,  notarius  publicus;"  but 
subsequent  attestations    run   thus: — "Clericus    Glasguensis    diocesis,   auctoritate 


PREFACE.  xi 

Apostolica   perprius,    notarius,   ac   nunc   per  dominos  concilii    admissus."     From 

the  act  of  admission  it  is  ascertained  that  Hegait  was  a  native  of  Glasgow, 
was  then  (1563-4)  forty-two  years  of  age,  and  held  the  appointment  of  Court 
Clerk  of  Glasgow;  and  it  is  probable  that  he  was  also  connected  with  the 
Barony  Court.  He  does  not  appear  to  have  been  a  priest,  as  so  many  of 
the  Papal  notaries  were;  but  having  been  admitted  by  the  Bishop  he 
would,  it  is  presumed,  be  entitled  to  exercise  his  office  in  any  part  of  the 
diocese.  At  what  period  Hegait  was  first  appointed  to  the  office  of  town 
clerk  has  not  been  ascertained,  as  the  existing  council  records  from  which 
the  information  might  have  been  obtained  do  not  commence  till  1573. 
Between  1574  and  1580  (when  another  long  hiatus  occurs)  his  name 
appears  yearly  on  the  leet  from  which  the  clerk  was  to  be  chosen;  but  on 
each  occasion  another  candidate  received  the  appointment.  In  the  privy 
council  register,  under  date  13th  December,  1564,  there  is  notice  of  a 
complaint  by  James  Law,  one  of  the  bailies,  against  "William  Hegait, 
common  clerk,  for  the  speaking  of  divers  despitefull  and  injurious  wordis 
to  the  said  James,  manifestly  contempnand  and  vilipendand  him,  being 
bailie  and  magistrate."  The  accused  official  denied  the  charge,  and  as  nothing 
farther  is  recorded  on  the  subject  it  may  be  assumed  that  matters  were 
satisfactorily   arranged. 

It  is  stated  in  his  act  of  admission  that  Hegait's  first  protocol  book 
commenced  on  2nd  October,  1544.  Only  a  few  torn  corners,  however,  now 
remain  of  the  first  nine  leaves,  and  the  book  commences  with  folio  10  and 
the  date  2nd  March,  1547-8.  With  the  exception  of  this  initial  mutilation, 
the  book  is  in  excellent  preservation  and  perfectly  legible.  As  already 
indicated,  the  transactions  recorded  in  it  principally  consist  of  attestations 
regarding  the  transfers  of  heritages.  When  a  sale  of  such  had  to  be  carried 
through,  and  the  property  happened  to  be  situated  within  the  bounds  of 
the  burgh,  the  seller  appeared  personally  on  the  ground,  in  presence  of  a 
bailie,  notary  and  witnesses,  and  stating  that  he  had  sold  the  property  to 
the  purchaser,  at  a  certain  price,  of  which  he  acknowledged  to  have  received 
payment,  resigned  the  property  into  the  hands  of  the  bailie,  by  delivering 
to  him  earth  and  stone  of  the  ground,  or  other  symbols  appropriate  to  the 
transaction.  Having  received  the  symbols,  the  bailie,  as  authorised  by  the 
seller,  and  in  virtue  of  his  office,  delivered  them  to  the  purchaser,  or  to 
an  attorney  acting  for  him,  and  thus  gave  him  sasine  or  possession  of 
his   piuxhase.      The    purchaser    then    asked   the   notary   to   prepare   an   instru- 


xii  PREFACE. 

ment  setting  forth  the  proceedings.  This  the  notary  did  by  recording  the 
particulars  in  his  protocol  book,  and  also  in  the  instrument,  which  latter 
was  delivered  to  the  purchaser,  and  retained  by  him  as  his  title  deed. 
"When  the  property  was  not  held  by  burgage  tenure,  there  was  produced  a 
precept  authorising  a  bailie  named  in  it — designated  a  bailie  "  in  hac  parte,"  i.e., 
in  that  part,  or  in  that  special  matter — to  give  sasine,  and  proceedings 
of  investiture,  similar  to  those  in  burgage  properties,  were  followed  (See 
2,  30,  42,  58,  &c.)  The  bailie  of  the  Subdean  of  Glasgow  seems  to  have 
had  the  privilege  of  giving  infeftment  in  lands  situated  in  the  Provanside 
district   (See   Nos.    25,    38,   211,    &c.) 

When  an  heir  desired  to  obtain  infeftment,  he  appeared  before  one  of  the 
bailies,  produced  evidence  of  propinquity,  and  humbly  required  the  bailie  to 
give  him  infeftment  in  the  property  of  which  his  ancestor  had  been  vested  at 
the  time  of  his  death.  The  bailie  having  found  the  claim  to  be  just  and 
reasonable,  proceeded  to  the  ground,  and  there  in  presence  of  a  notary  and 
witnesses   gave   sasine   in   the   usual   manner. 

The  symbols  used  in  giving  sasine  both  for  lands  and  tenements  are 
usually  earth  and  stone,  though  for  tenements  hasp  and  staple  (i.e., 
the  latch,  lock,  or  other  fastenings  of  the  door)  are  occasionally  mentioned. 
In  protocol  No.  17  the  symbols  for  the  two  tenements  and  yards  are 
earth  and  stone,  and  for  the  annualrent  hasp  and  staple.  Hasp  and  staple 
were  the  usual  symbols  for  infeftment  in  annualrents  payable  from 
tenements,  e.g.  Nos.  28,  31,  65,  157,  186.  Less  frequently,  as  in  No.  197,  the 
symbol  was  a  penny,  and  in  No.  35  the  symbols  were  put  alternatively  hasp 
and  staple  or  a  penny.  When  annualrents  were  payable  from  lands  in  the 
country,  as  in  Nos.  235  to  237,  the  symbol  was  a  penny.  In  No.  59  the 
symbols  were  earth  and  stone  for  the  lands,  and  net  and  coble  for  the 
fishings;  and  in  No.  120  sasine  was  given  in  property  in  Kilbryde  parish  by 
delivery   of   rafter   and   rigging   of   the   buildings. 

In  the  Appendix  a  few  protocols  are  given  in  extenso  for  the  purpose 
of  showing  the  style  of  the  protocols,  so  that  the '  reader  may  gather 
from  them  the  mode  adopted  in  framing  the  abstracts.  An  abstract  of 
every  protocol  is  given,  and  the  descriptions  of  properties  and  names  and 
designations  of  individuals  are  invariably  preserved  intact,  the  aim  being 
to  present  the  essence  of  the  original,  and  only  to  leave  out  the  formalities. 
The  protocols,  it  will  be  observed,  begin  with  a  statement  of  the  date,  the 
indiction,    and   the  pontificate.      The   two    latter    particulars,    instead    of   being 


PREFACE.  xiii 

repeated  each  time  in  the  abstracts,  have  been  printed  at  the  top  of  the 
page.  News  from  Rome  in  these  days  appears  from  the  state  of  the  notary's 
information  on  Papal  affairs  to  have  been  often  dilatory  and  sometimes 
unreliable.  Thus  though  Pope  Paul  III.  died  on  10th  November,  1549,  his 
name  appears  in  the  protocols  up  to  27th  February,  1549-50.  On  the  5th 
of  the  following  month  there  is  the  memorandum  "Romana  curia  uti  relatum 
erat  pastore  carente,"  and  a  similar  note  is  repeated  in  the  protocols  till 
26th  July.  Julius  III.,  who  was  elected  on  8th  February,  1549-50,  and 
died  5th  March,  1554-5,  first  appears  in  the  protocols  on  18th  August,  1550, 
and  his  name  is  still  continued  on  14th  May,  1555.  Paul  IV.  was  elected 
on  23rd  May,  1555,  and  his  pontificate  is  first  noticed  in  the  protocols  on 
5th  June  thereafter,  but  under  the  misnomer  of  Julius  IV.  In  the  Abstracts 
these  mistakes  and  any  other  clerical  slips  are  not  taken  into  account,  the 
correct  year  both  of  pontificate  and  indiction  being  given.  The  Indiction 
current  during  the  period  embraced  in  the  Abstracts  commenced  on  24th 
September,    1542. 

The  greater  number  of  the  protocols  refer  to  properties  in  the  city  of 
Glasgow,  then  a  town  of  moderate  dimensions.  The  main  thoroughfare,  stretching 
from  the  Market  Cross  to  the  Cathedral  Church,  had  dwellings  and  booths  on 
each  side,  the  number  on  the  west  preponderating,  as  on  the  opposite  side 
considerable  space  was  taken  up  by  the  Blackfriars  Convent  and  College  buildings 
and  grounds.  Southward  from  the  Cross  a  street  extended  to  the  South  Port 
and  then  branched  off  obliquely  towards  the  bridge.  Again  starting  from  the 
Cross  the  Gallowgait  extended  eastward  a  little  beyond  the  Molendinar  Burn, 
and  the  Trongait  westward,  with  a  prolongation  towards  St.  Tenu's  Chapel. 
There  were  few  buildings  in  the  Gallowgait  on  the  east  side  of  the  Molendinar, 
while  those  on  the  north  side  of  the  Trongait  did  not  extend  farther  than 
the  West  Port.  There  were  a  few  houses  in  Stockwellgait,  and  a  turreted 
building  at  the  northwest  corner.  At  least  one  wynd  extended  from  Tron- 
gait to  Bridgegait,  and  there  were  also  wynds  or  vennels  on  each  side  of 
the  High  Street.  From  the  Wyndhead,  otherwise  called  the  Quadrivium, 
the  Ratounraw  branched  out  on  the  west  and  the  Drygait  on  the  east,  and 
these  streets  were  largely  occupied  by  the  dwellings  of  church  dignitaries. 
In  1587  the  freemen  and  inhabitants  of  that  locality,  in  a  supplication  to 
parliament,  represented  that  the  part  of  the  city  situated  above  the  Greyfriars 
Wynd,  "afoir  the  reformation  of  the  religioun,  wes  intertenyt  and  uphalden 
be    the    resort    of    the    Bischop,   Parsonis,  and   Vicaris,"   and    was    "the    onlie 


xiv  PREFACE. 

ornament  of  the  citie,  be  ressone  of  the  grite  and  sumptuous  buildings  of 
grite  antiquitie,  varie  proper  and  meit  for  the  ressait  of  his  Hienes  and 
nobilitie  at  sic  tymes  as  thai  sail  repair  thairto."1  A  few  isolated  dwellings 
might  be  scattered  here  and  there  beyond  the  range  of  the  streets  above 
enumerated,  but,  subject  to  such  exceptions,  the  built  area  of  the  city  was 
confined  within  the  limits  indicated.  Dr.  Cleland,  in  his  Statistical  Account 
of  Glasgow,  estimated  that  the  population  of  the  town  at  the  time  of  the 
Reformation   amounted    to   about   4500. 

With  the  view  of  conveying  a  better  idea  of  the  topographical  features 
of  the  city,  and  making  the  descriptions  in  the  protocols  more  intelligible, 
the  accompanying  Sketch  Plan  has  been  prepared  by  Mr.  M'Donald,  city 
engineer.  Further  information  on  many  points  is,  however,  desiderated  in  order 
that  the  plan  might  be  made  more  specific.  Subsequent  protocols  will  doubtless 
reveal  much  that  is  at  present  unknown,  and  confirm  or  demolish  doubtful 
theories.  Poldrait  Burn,  which  it  is  believed  has  not  previously  been 
delineated  on  any  plan  of  Glasgow,  has  been  laid  down  from  a  consideration 
of  the  two  or  three  references  to  it  in  the  protocols,  coupled  with  inferences 
drawn  from  the  configuration  of  ground  in  the  vicinity;  but  other  descriptions 
may  determine  the  precise  position  of  the  stream.  The  sites  of  the  chapel 
of  St.  Roche  and  its  cemetery,  and  of  the  Greyfriars  Convent,  with  the 
positions  of  some  of  the  crofts,  may  also  be  settled  with  greater  precision 
when  subsequent  protocols  are  published.  The  "burn  called  Glasgw,"  which 
flowed  through  a  verdant  holm,  between  its  source  near  the  church  of  St. 
Kentigern  and  its  outlet  a  few  yards  beyond  the  reputed  tomb  of  St.  Tenu, 
has  hitherto  appeared  on  maps  as  St.  Enoch's  Burn;  but  "Glasgow"  is  the 
original  name,  and  one  which  occasionally  appears  in  title  deeds  at  the 
present  day.  Perhaps  these  facts  have  hitherto  escaped  notice,  or,  at  all 
events,  have  not  received  due  -prominence  in  discussions  on  the  origin  of 
the   name   of   the   city. 

At  the  end  of  Hegait's  first  volume,  after  the  last  protocol,  are  five 
leaves,  on  the  first  page  of  which  Robert  Scott,  director  of  chancery, 
has  written  his  certificate  stating  the  contents  (p.  88).  The  pen  also  has 
been  drawn  diagonally  through  this  and  each  of  the  six  succeeding  pages 
which  are  otherwise  blank,  except  that  at  the  top  of  the  7th  page  there 
is  scribbled   "R.  Doune  the  said  Johne."     On  page  8  there  is  a  memorandum, 

1  Glasgow  Charters,  Part  II.,  p.  213. 


PREFACE.  xv 

written  in  Latin,  bearing  that  on  21st  October,  1546,  Hugh  Monthgumry  of 
Hasilheid  delivered  to  Thomas  Monthgumry  of  Skelmurlie,  a  letter  of  bailiary, 
dated  at  Irvyne,  17th  August  preceding;  another  letter  of  the  bailiary  of 
Stewartoune  of  the  same  date;  a  letter  of  assignation  of  the  ten  pound  land 
of  Skelmurlie,  the  eight  pound  land  of  Loichlokerthsyide  in  the  sheriffdom  of 
Renfrew,  the  twenty  merk  land  of  Loichransay,  and  the  twelve  merk  land  of 
Sannokis,  within  the  Isle  of  Arrane  and  shire  of  Bute;  and  a  contract  between 
George  Monthgumry  of  Scelmurlie  and  John  Monthgumry  of  Langschaw,  dated 
at  Irvyne,  16th  August,  in  the  same  year.  Following  the  memorandum  is  a 
letter  from  Hegait  to  a  brother  notary,  which  runs  thus: — "Brethir, — Pleis  yow 
ressave  this  my  prothogoll  buk  ye  wrait  for.  Concernyng  Dike,  that  fallow,  I 
salbe  at  yow  schortlie  and  satifieyour  lawbour,  God  willing,  quha  haif  yow  in 
his  protectioune. — Your  brother,  W.  Hegait.  To  my  brother  Maister  James 
Donaldesoun  delivyr  this."  There  is  no  marking  on  page  9.  The  last  page 
is  nearly  covered  with  writing,  but  being  much  soiled  and  slightly  mutilated, 
the  moral  precepts  and  maxims  of  which  it  seems  to  be  composed  cannot  be 
very  well  made  out  connectedly.  An  admonition  in  favour  of  "vertuus  levyng 
and  in  doing  of  gud"  can  be  only  partially  deciphered,  while  another,  more 
legible,  reminds  one  somewhat  of  a   passage  in   Burns : — 

"Aye  free,  aff  han',  your  story  tell, 
When  wi'  a  bosom  crony; 
But  still  keep  something  to  yoursel' 
Ye  scarcely  tell  to  ony." 

The  Protocol  Book  version  runs  thus : — "Reveile  nevir  the  secreit  of  thine 
mynde  to  na  maner  of  persone  except  yow  wit  weile  he  is  thine  verray 
freind  and  will  nocht  reveile  it  agane."  Following  upon  this  cautious  counsel 
comes  the  injunction,  "Speik  evill  of  na  maner  of  man  nor  woman,  nowder 
absent  nor  present," — an  excellent  precept  and  presumably  needed  in  the  six- 
teenth  century. 

There  are  in  the  city's  archives  forty-one  protocol  books  in  all,  embracing  the 
period  from  1547  to  1696.  From  1694  onward  there  is  a  regular  series  of  Sasine 
Registers,  which  superseded  the  protocol  books.  These  number  at  the  present 
time  close  upon  one  thousand  volumes,  and  are  increasing  at  the  rate  of 
sixteen  volumes  annually.  It  is  proposed  that  the  publication  of  abstracts  of 
the  protocols  should  be  brought  down  to  at  least  the  year  1600.  There  are 
ten   MS.  volumes  applicable  to  that  period,  and  it  is  intended  that  subsequent 


xvi  PREFACE. 

publications    should    bo    of    such  ;i  size    as  to   admit   of  the   abstracts  of  these 
being   contained   in   not   more  than   three   volumes. 

The  Editor  is  indebted  to  Mr.  David  Donaldson  for  the  carefully  prepared 
Index,  embracing  every  name  which  occurs  in  the  abstracts.  To  Mr.  George 
Neilson,  F.S.A.,  Scot.,  he  has  also  to  offer  acknowledgment  for  the  assistance 
rendered  by  him  while  the  book  was  passing  through  the  press.  Mr.  Neilson 
read  all  the  proof  sheets  and  made  many  valuable  suggestions  which  have 
been  duly  taken  advantage  of,  and  altogether  the  work  has  in  many  ways 
benefited  by  the  discriminating  attention  he  bestowed  upon  it. 

R.    RENWICK. 

Glasgow,  November,  1894.

IV. — Procotol  relating  to  the  Transfer  of  an  Annualrent  on  Sale. 
(Abstract  No.  31). 

Blakburne — Wreicht. 

Die  tercio  mensis  Aprilis  anno  Domini,  etc.,  j"'  vc  1",  indictione  octava, 
Romana  curia  pastore  carente.  In  mei  notarii  publici  et  testium  subscriptorum 
presentiis  personaliter  constituti  discrete  persone  Patricius  Wreicht  et  Cristina 
Androsoune,  conjuges,  existentes  in  statu  et  saisina  unius  tenementi  tam  ante 
quam  retro  cum  singulis  pertinenciis  jaeentis  infra  territorium  civitatis  Glasguensis 
in  via  tendente  a  loco  Fratrum  Predicatorum  ad  Crucem  Foralem,  inter 
terras  quondam  Roberta  Walkar  nunc  vero  Nove  Ecclesie  spectantes  ex 
parte  boreali,  tenementum  Joannis  Wan  ex  parte  australi,  et  terras  sive 
hortum  David  Lyndesay  ex  parte  occidentali,  et  communem  viam  regiam  ex 
parte  orientali;  qui  vero  Patricius  et  Cristina,  conjuges,  unanimi  consensu  et 
assensu,  ac  non  vi  aut  metu  ducti,  nee  errore  lapsi,  compulsi  aut  coacti, 
sed   ex   suis    meris,    liberis,    et    spontaneis   voluntatibus,   utilitate    et     commodo

suis  et  suonini  penitus  provisis  et  pensatis,  vendiderunt  ac  titulo  pure  venditionis 
a   se   heredibus   suis    et   assignatis   pro    perpetuo    alienaverunt   .honorabili    viro 
Archinbaldo  Blakburne,  heredibus  suis  et  assignatis,  hereditarie  totum  et  integrum

dictorum    Patricii     et    Cristine     totaliter    conversa,     de    qua     iidem     conjuges

a  se  heredibus  suis  et  assignatis  in  manus  honorabilis  viri  Andrie  Mwre,  alterius 
ballivorum    civitatis   Glasguensis,    per   deliberationem   de    ly   hespe    et    stapile    in 
favorem    dicti     Archinbaldi    heredum    suorum    et    assignatorum    resignavit    ac 
totum   jus   et   clameum.       Qua    resignatione   sic   facta   et   per   dictum   ballivum

heredibus  suis  et  assignatis  per  redeliberationem  dictorum  ly  hesp  et  stapile  dedit,

noil    fuit    compulsa    aut    coacta, '  per    vim    aut    nietum   dicti    sui   sponsi   extra

V. — Procotol  relating  to  the  Resignation  of  Property  in  the  hands 
of  the  Bailie  of  the  Subdean  of  Glasgow.     (Abstract  No.  200). 

MWRE   AND    HERBISOUN. 

Eodem  die  horam  circiter  undecimam  ante  merediem  Alexander  Mwre, 
ballivus  subdecani,  dedit  statum  et  saisinam  hereditarios,  necnon  realem 
actualem  et  corporalem  possessionem  unius  acre  terre  jacentis  in  Provandesyide, 
jacentis  inter  terras  nunc  occupatas  per  Johannem  Mwre  ex  parte  occidentali 
et  terras  capellanie  Sancti  Kenthigerni  ex  parte  orientali;  necnon  aliarum 
duarura  rigarum  terrarum  campestrium  ibidem  jacentium  inter  terras  occupatas 
per  Willelmum  Donaldesoune  spectantes  capellanie  Sancti  Kenthigerni  ex 
occidentali  et  terras  dicte  capellanie  spectantes  ex  orientali  [Mergarete  Mure] 
tanquam  legittimo  heredi  quondam  Osualdi  Mwre  uti  inventum  erat  per 
wardam  curie  civitatis  Glasguensis  condignam.  Dicta  Mergareta  resignavit 
hujusmodi  acram  cum  dictis  duabus  rigis  in  manus  dicti  Alexandri,  ballivi 
antedicti,  pro  hereditaria  saisina  sibi  et  dicto  Georgio,  et  ipsorum  alteri 
diutius  viventi,  in  conjuncta  infeodatione  heredibus  suis  et  assignatis  quibus- 
cumque;  qua  resignatione  sic  facta  et  per  dictum  ballivum  admissa  et  recepta, 
idem  ballivus  statum  et  saisinam  hereditarios,  necnon  realem,  actualem,  et 
corporalem  possessionem  dicte  acre  terre,  cum  dictis  duabus  rigis  terre, 
singulatim  et  per  se,  dictis  Mergarete  et  Georgio  Herbisoune,  conjugibus, 
heredibus  suis  et  assignatis  prescriptis,  per  deliberationem  terre  et  lapidis  fundi 
earundem,  singillatim  et  per  se,  uti  moris  est,  dedit,  tradidit  pariter  et 
deliberavit.  Super  quibus  singulis  dictus  Georgius  et  Mergareta  petierunt 
instrumenta.  Acta  erant  hec  super  fundum  prescriptarum  terrarum,  singillatim 
et  per  se,  coram  his  testibus,  viz.,  Johanne  Sellar,  Archinbaldo  Mwre, 
Cuthtberto  Herbisoune,  burgense  Glasguense,  domino  David  M'Kownne,  notario, 
et   David   YVatsoune,   seriando,  cum   diversis   aliis.

3.  Lyoun — Summer.      5  April,  1548,  10  a.m. 

Bartholomew  Summer  and  Cristina  Cunyghame,  spouses,  (for  relieving  the 
said  Bartholomew  of  debts  owing  to  sundry  creditors,  for  which  he  was  bound 
under  acts  inserted  in  the  books  of  the  lord  official  of  Glasgow,  and  in  the  rolls 
of  the  burgh  court,  for  non-implement  of  which  acts  he  had  been  incarcerated), 
with  express  consent  of  John  Cunyghame,  father  of  the  said  Cristina,  at  whose 
instance  he  was  interdicted  from  alienating  or  mortgaging  his  property,  sold  to 
David  Lyon  and  Jonet  Campbell,  spouses,  and  longest  liver  of  them,  their  fore 
tenements  and  pertinents,  lying  within  the  territory  of  the  city,  in  the  street 
passing  from  the  Market  Cross  to  the  place  of  the  Friars  Preachers,  between 
the  lands  of  Sir  Andrew  Heriot,  chaplain,  on  the  north,  the  lands  or  tenement 
of  William  Smith  on  the  south,  the  back  tenement  of  the  said  Bartholomew  on 
the  west,  and  the  street  on  the  east.  Sasine  given  by  Andrew  Dunlop,  bailie. 
Witnesses:  Master  Nicolas  Wethirspwne,  George  Rwnen,  Patrick  Sempell,  and 
Archibald  Lyon.

the  tenement  of  the  deceased  Archibald  Mwre  on  the  north,  the  tenement  of  q.

8.  Masone — Houstone.      13  August,  1549. 

Sir  John  Masone,  vicar  of  the  choir  of  the  metropolitan  church  of  Glasgow, 
gave  to  John  Houstone  and  Elizabeth  Masone,  his  spouse,  and  sister  of  Sir  John, 
and  their  heirs  (whom  failing  the  property  to  revert  to  the  granter's  heirs), 
(1)  piece  of  yard  and  waste  tenement  lying  contiguous,  between  the  lands  of 
James  Fare  and  of  Archibald  Wilsone  on  the  north,  a  small  vennel  on  the 
south,  the  newly  built  lodging  in  which  Sir  David  Masone  now  dwells  on  the 
east,  and  the  other  part  of  the  said  yard  on  the  west;  the  grantees  paying  to 
Sir  John  13s.  4d.  yearly.  (2)  The  said  newly  built  lodging  in  which  the  said 
Sir  David  dwells,  lying  t.c.  between  the  said  piece  of  waste  tenement  on  the  west, 
the  tenement  of  q.  John  Wynzet  on  the  east,  the  common  vennel  on  the  south, 
and  another  small  vennel  on  the  north;  reserving  nevertheless  to  Sir  John  and 
Sir  David  the  said  lodging  during  their  lifetimes.  Sasine  given  by  Mr.  John 
Hall,  bailie.  Witnesses:  William  Wat,  John  Johnstoune,  burgesses  of  Glasgow; 
George   Brounsyde,    servitor  to   William   Hegait,  and   James  Dalgleis,  Serjeant.

the    said   Jonet  sasine  of    (1)  a   fore   booth,   with    booth  adjoining,  cellars,   and

13.  Lyndesay.  22  November,  1549. 

John  Dike  and  Jonet  Gayne,  spouses,  sold  to  William  Lyndesay,  their 
fore  and  back  tenement,  with  yard,  close  and  pertinents,  lying  t.c.  in  the 
street  which  leads  from  the  South  Port  to  the  bridge  of  Clyde,  on  the  south 
side  thereof,  between  the  lands  of  John  Wedrop  on  the  west,  the  lands  of 
.  .  .  on  the  east,  the  Malindinor  Burn  on  the  south,  and  the  street  on  the 
north.  Sasine  given  by  John  Hall,  bailie.  Thereafter,  the  said  Jonet,  in 
presence  of  the  notary,  bailie,  and  witnesses,  gave  her  bodily  oath,  on  the  holy 
evangels  of  God,  that  she  was  not  compelled  or  coacted  to  this  sale  and 
resignation.  Witnesses:  William  Roger,  burgess  of  Glasgow;  David  Watsoune, 
John  Rob. 

14.  Brwme.  24  November,    1549,  2  p.m. 

David  Wilsoune,  procurator,  and  in  name  of  Marion  Brwme,  acting  under 
letters  of  procuratory,  dated  5  November,  1549,  resigned  in  favor  of  her 
kinsman,  Robert  Brwne,  citizen  of  Glasgow,  a  tenement,  with  yard  and 
two  rigs  of  land,  and  pertinents,  lying  near  (prope)  the  city  of  Glasgow, 
in  the  street  of  St.  Tenu,  between  the  lands  or  tenements  of  Cuthbert 
Blakuod  on  the  east,  the  lands  of  John  Wan  on  the  west  of  the  tenement, 
the  lands  of  Sir  Andrew  Burell  on  the  west  of  the  rigs,  and  other  lands  on 
the  east  of  the  rigs;  and  that  for  a  certain  sum  of  money  paid  by  the  said 
Robert  to  the  procurator.  The  letters  of  procuratory  bear  to  be  sealed  with 
the  seal  of  office  of  the  bailie  of  Glasgow,  because  the  granter  had  not  a 
seal  of  her  own,  and  to  be  signed  by  "Marion  Brwme,  with  my  hand  led 
at  the  pen"  by  David  M'Corne,  notary  public,  because  she  could  not  write; 
all  in  presence  of  Mr.  Nicolas  Wetherspwne,  John  Andersoun,  and  Robert 
Miller,  burgesses  of  the  city.  Sasine  given  by  John  Mwre,  bailie.  Witnesses: 
John   Seller,     .     .     .     Wedrop,    and   James    Howe.

At  the  request  of  John  Robisoune,  son  and  heir  of  the  late  George 
Robisoune,  John  Mwre,  bailie,  invested  him  with  power  to  redeem  a  piece 
of   fore   land,  lying   in   the   street  which  leads   from   the   Market  Cross  to   the

South  Port,  on  the  east  side  thereof,  between  the  lands  of  Michael  Lyndesay 
on  the  south,  the  lands  of  Mr.  John  Sprewle  on  the  north,  and  the  lands 
of  Robert  Reche  on  the  east,  conform  to  the  tenor  of  letters  of  reversion 
relating  thereto;  and  the  said  John  thereupon  assigned  all  his  right  in  the 
tenement,  by  virtue  of  the  said  letters  of  reversion,  to  Elizabeth  Galbrayth,

burgess,   William    Heriot,   burgess;    John   Seller  and  David  Watsoun,   Serjeants. 

16.  CRAWFURDE.  18  December,   1549. 

Archibald  Crawfurde,  burgess  of  the  city  of  Glasgow,  resigned  in  favor 
of  Archibald  Crawfurde,  his  son,  a  tenement  lying  t.c.  on  the  north  side  of 
the  Ratounraw  (via  Batonum),  between  the  lands  or  tenement  of  the  vicars 
of  the  choir  on  the  east,  the  lands  of  q.  Alexander  Crawfurde  on  the  west, 
the  lands  of  q.  .  .  .  on  the  north,  and  the  street  on  the  south.  Sasine 
given  by  John  Mwre,  bailie.  Witnesses:  John  Wedrop,  burgess;  John  Seller 
and   David   Watsoun,    Serjeants. 

17.  Mr.  Knox.  18  December,   1549,   11  a.m. 

John  Hall,  bailie,  gave  sasine  to  Archinbakl  Knox,  as  heir  of  q.  David 
Knox,  burgess  of  the  city,  his  brother,  of  (1)  a  tenement  and  yard  lying 
t.c.  in  the  Tronegait,  between  the  lands  of  q.  John  Aitkyn  and  William 
Lowthyane  on  the  west,  the  lands  of  q.  John  Masoune  on  the  east  of  the 
tenement,  the  yard  of  q.  John  and  Henry  Burell  on  the  east  of  the  yard, 
and  the  street  on  the  south;  (2)  a  tenement,  with  yard  and  pertinents,  on  that 
side  (citra)  the  Malyndinor  Burn  and  bridge,  commonly  called  "the  Gallowgait 
Brige,"  between  the  lands  of  Alexander  Stevyn  on  the  west,  the  lands  of 
Lowik  on  the  east,  the  Malyndinor  Burn  on  the  north,  and  the 
burn  commonly  called  Poldrait  on  the  south;  (3)  an  annualrent  of  twenty 
shillings  yearly,  payable  furth  of-  a  tenement  of  David  Wyle,  lying  in  the 
Tronegait,  between  the  lands  of  q.  John  Park  on  the  east,  the  lands  of 
Mr.  Robert  Hill  on  the  west,  and  the  street  on  the  north.  Thereafter,  the 
said  Archibald,  in  his  urgent  necessity,  and  for  a  certain  sum  of  money  paid 
to  him,  sold  the  said  tenements  and  others  to  John  Mwre  and  Margaret 
Ranking,  spouses.  Sasine  given  by  Mr.  John  Hall,  bailie.  Witnesses:  James 
Mwre,    Robert   Rob,   and   John   Seller.

20.  Gibsoune.  4  February,  1549-50,   10  a.m. 

James  Gibsoune,  citizen  of  Glasgow,  resigned  in  favor  of  Marion  Gibsoune,

.  .  .  merks.  Sasine  given  by  Mr.  John  Hall,  bailie.  Witnesses:  William

21.  Ker— Gilcrist.  5  February,  1549-50. 

Elizabeth  Ker  resigned  in  favor  of  herself  and  John  Gilcrist,  her  spouse,

Hall,  bailie.  Witnesses:  John  Mwre,  bailie;  John  Rob,  David  Lyoun,  William 
Wilsoun,  burgesses,  and  John  Seller,  serjeant.

23.  Coichrane — Crawfurde — Hammyltoune.     27  Feb.,  1549-50,  10  a.m. 
John   Coichran,    heir   of   q.    Ninian    Knox,    sometime    citizen   of    Glasgow, 

presented  to  John  Mwre,  bailie,  a  precept  of  sasine  granted  by  James  Stewart, 
canon  of  the  Metropolitan  Church  of  Glasgow,  and  commissioner  of  Mr.  Gavin 
Hammyltoune,  dean  of  the  church  and  vicar  general  of  the  vacant  see  thereof, 
setting  forth  that,  by  a  process  led  in  presence  of  the  lords  of  council  of 
our  Sovereign  Lady  Mary,  by  the  grace  of  God,  Queen  of  Scots,  and  their 
decreet  thereon,  it  was  found  that  q.  Ninian  Knox,  citizen  of  Glasgow,  and 
brother  german  of  q.  Katharine  Knox,  mother  of  John  Coichran,  bearer  of 
the  precept,  died  last  vest  and  seised  as  of  fee,  at  the  peace  and  faith  of 
our  Sovereign  Lady  the  Queen,  in  a  tenement,  fore  and  back,  with  a  yard 
and  acre  of  land  lying  t.c.  on  the  east  side  of  the  street  which  leads  from 
the  place  of  the  Preaching  Friars  to  the  cross  streets  (Quadrivium);  between 
the  lands  of  q.  George  Carlile  on  the  north,  the  tenement  of  John  Hawik 
on  the  south,  and  the  Malyndinor  Burn  on  the  east;  and  that  the  said  John 
Coichran  was  lawful'  and  nearest  heir  of  the  said  Ninian  Knox,  in  the  said 
lands,  tenement,  yard  and  acre  of  land,  with  the  pertinents;  therefore,  the 
provost  and  bailies  of  the  city  of  Glasgow  were  charged  to  give  sasine  thereof 
to  the  said  John  Coichran.  The  precept  was  given  under  the  seal  of  office  of 
the  vicar  general  of  Glasgow,  on  19  February,  1549.  After  the  reading  of  the 
precept  the  bailie  gave  sasine  in  terras  thereof.  Witnesses:  James  Edmestoun 
of  Bellewyn;  John  Boyle,  son  of  the  laird  of  Kelburne;  James  Lyndesay, 
Sir  David  Hammyltoune,  provost  of  the  Collegiate  Church  of  Dunbartane; 
William   Donaldsoune,    John    Mertyne,    and   John   Seller,    serjeant.

30.  Maxwell — Pollok.     28  March,  1550,  between  6  and  7  a.m.

Queen  Mary,  under  the  great  seal,  dated  13  March,  1549,1  granting  to  the 
said  John  and  his  spouse  and  1.1.  the  six  merk  lands  of  old  extent  of  Heid- 
houssis  and  Murrayis,  with  the  pertinents.  After  the  reading  of  the  precept, 
the  sheriff  gave  sasine  to  the  grantees  by  delivery  of  earth  and  stone. 
Done  on  the  ground  of  the  lands,  at  the  principal  messuage  thereof.  Witnesses: 
James  Foret,  burgess  of  the  city  of  Glasgow,  Roger  Kirkpatrik,  William  Dik, 
one  of   the  tenants  or  tillers  of   the  said   lands,  and     ....     Jaksoun.- 

31.  Blakbukne — Wreicht.     3  April,  1550,  10  a.m. 

Patrick  Wreicht  and  Cristina  Androsoune,  spouses,  sold  to  Archinbald 
Blakburne  an  annualrent  of  30  s.,  payable  furth  of  their  tenement  lying  in 
the  street  leading  from  the  place  of  the  Preaching  Friars  to  the  Market 
Cross,  between  the  lands  of  q.  Robert  Walker,  then  belonging  to  the  New 
Church,  on  the  north,  the  tenement  of  John  Wan  on  the  south,  the  lands 
or  yard  of  David  Lyndesay  on  the  west,  and  the  street  on  the  east.  Ratified 
by  the  wife  in  absence  of  her  husband.  Witnesses:  John  Huchesoune,  burgess, 
Sir  David   M'Kowne,  notary,  and  David  Watsoune,  serjeant.

32.  Pollok — Maxuell — Lyndesay — Dunrod.     Eodem  die  et  hora. 

Mr.  David  Wilsoune,  procurator,  and  in  name  of  Elizabeth  Maxuell,  and 
John  Maxuell  of  Nethir  Pollok,  for  his  interest,  appeared  in  presence  of 
Robert  Lyndesay  of  Dunrod,  and  premonished  him  to  compear  in  the  parish 
church  of  Rutherglen,  and  there,  upon  the  high  altar,  receive  a  certain  sum 
of  money  for  the  lawful  redemption  of  the  lands  of  Murrayis  and  Heidhows, 
conform  to  letters  of  reversion  made  thereon.  Witnesses:  Robert  Stewarde 
in  Neilstounesyde,  John  Stewarde  of  Gillycroy,  Thomas  Leiche,  and  Gabriele 
Sempill  of  Graigbait.  Dene  within  the  city  of  Glasgow.  Thereafter,  Robert 
Lyndesay  requested  Mr.  David  Wilsoune  that  he  should  name  to  him  the 
place  of  the  said  church  of  Rutherglen  where  the  sum  of  money  is  to  be 
paid  for  redemption  of  the  said  lands.  Mr.  David  named  the  high  altar, 
and  the  said  Robert  then  instantly  denied  that  any  reversion  had  been  granted 
by   any  person   whomsoever   concerning   the   redemption    of   the   said   lands.

Aitkyn,  her  father,  of  a  tenement  lying  in  the  Trongait,  between  the  lands

lands  of  Langcroft  on  the  north,  and  the  street  on  the  south.  Thereafter, 
the  said  Jonet  resigned  the  tenement  in  favor  of  David  Bertane,  her  husband, 
and  herself  and  1.1.  Thereafter,  the  spouses  sold  to  Nicholas  Androw  and 
.  .  .  .  Lyndesay,  spouses,  the  fore  tenement,  with  eighteen  feet  in  length 
on  the  east  side  of  the  close,  and  one  half  of  the  breadth  of  the  closs,  with 
free  ingress  and  egress  by  the  back  gate  to  the  yard  of  the  said  Nicholas; 
reserving  to  the  said  David  and  Jonet  an  annualrent  of  30s.  Ratified  by 
Jonet  Aitken  in  absence  of  her  husband.  Witnesses:  James  Andersoun, 
William  Wat,  burgess;  Nicolas  Bog,  Constantine  Browne,  and  David  Watsoune,

35.  Summyk.  12  April,  1550,  betw.  2  and  3  p.m. 

Mr.  John  Hall,  bailie,  gave  sasine  to  Adam  Summer  as  heir  of  Bartholomew 
Summyr,  his  brother,  of  the  lands,  tenements,  and  annualrents  underwritten, 
viz.:  (1)  a  barn,  commonly  called  "ane  bernesteid,"  lying  in  the  front  of  the 
Longe  Crofte,  in  the  street  of  Sanct  Tenu,  between  the  lands  of  dame  Fulbar 
on  the  east,  and  the  lands  of  the  Collegiate  Church  of  the  Blessed  Virgin 
Mary,  then  occupied  by  David  Lyndesay,  on  the  west;  (2)  a  "kilsteid,"  lying 
in  said  street,  between  the  lands  of  John  Wan  on  the  west,  the  common 
streets  on  the  east  and  south,  and  the  lands  of  the  said  Bartholomew  on 
the  north;  (3)  five  roods  or  rigs  of  lands  in  the  Pavillioun  Croft,  between 
the  lands  of  the  said  John  Wan  on  the  west,  a  vennel  called  the  Lone 
on  the  east,  the  lands  of  Medowflat  on  the  north,  and  a  kilsteid  on  the 
south;  (4)  an  annualrent  of  four  shillings,  payable  furth  of  a  "bernesteid," 
with  the  pertinents  belonging  to  James  Will,  lying  in  St.  Tenu's  Croft,  between 
the  lands  of  the  chaplainry  of  St.  Nicolas  on  the  east,  and  the  lands  of  q. 
John  AVidrof  on  the  west;  (5)  a  rig  of  land  lying  in  the  said  croft,  between 
the  lands  of  the  vicars  of  the  choir  of  the  city  of  Glasgow  on  the  west, 
and  the  lands  of  q.  David  Munteitht  on  the  east;  (6)  an  annualrent  of  seven 
shillings,  payable  furth  of  a  tenement  sometime  belonging  to  William  Walkar, 
between  the  lands  of  q.  William  Andersoune  on  the  south,  and  the  lands 
of  Bartholomew  Symsoune  on  the  north;  (7)  two  rigs  or  roods  of  open  lands 
lying  in  Eglisemis  Croft,  between  the  lands  of  St.  James  on  the  west,  and 
the   lands  of   the  Collegiate  Church   of  the  Blessed  Virgin   Mary  on  the   east; 

(8)  a  rig  of  open  land  lying  in  Kynclayth,  between  the  lands  of  Archinbald 
Blakburne  on  the  east,  and  the   lands  of  the  vicars  of  the  choir  on  the  west; 

(9)  an  annual  rent  of  4s.,  payable  furth  of  a  tenement  sometime  belonging 
to  John  Knox,  lying  in  the  Tronegait,  between  the  lands  of  q.  John  Masoune 
on  the  west,  and  the  lands  of  q.  John  Wedrop  on  the  east.  Witnesses :  David 
Lyndesay  of  Kittoksyide,  John  Robertoune  of  Ernok,  David  Lyoun,  Mr.

Summyr,    and   Archibald   Kneland.

We,  Williame  Hall,  Johne  Wan,  Williame  Wat,  and  Thomas  Androsoune, 
devyideris  chosyn  be  the  baillies  of  the  toune  and  ciete  of  Glesgw,  with

Johne  Masoune  upone  the  west  pairt,  the  land  and  tenement  of  wmquhill 
Johne  Wedrop  upone  the  est  pairt,  the  commowne  streit  upon  the  sowtht, — 
We,  the  saidis  persouneis  chosyn  judiciallie  as  said  is  for  devisioune  of  the 
saidis  tenement,  and  the  saidis  Elizabetht  and  Alexander  Stewarde,  hir  spous, 
for  his  entres,  and  the  saidis  Marioune  and  Archinbalde  Lyoun,  hir  spous, 
for  his  entres,  and  the  saidis  Jonet  personallie  for  hir  self,  compeirand  all 
personally  in  jugment,  and  gifand  their  bodely  aithtis  to  stand  at  oure 
devisioune,  but  revocatioune  or  reclamatioun,  as  at  mair  lintht  is  contenit 
in  ane  act  of  compromis  maid  and  gifin  thairupone.  And  thairefter,  we,  the 
saidis  devyderis,  compeirand  upone  the  grunde  of  the  said  tenement,  with 
yairde  and  pertinentis,  and  thair  accepting  the  divisioun  of  the  said  tenement, 
bayth  bak  land  and  foirland,  with  yairde  and  pertinentis,  in  and  upone  us, 
and  being  sworne  to  devyde  the  samyn  leily  and  trewlie  efter  our  undir- 
standing,  all  in  ane  voce  devyidis  the  said  land  and  tenement  as  eftir 
followis:  Item,  in  the  first,  we  ordane  the  foir  uvyr  hows,  viz.,  hall,  chalmer 
and  wairdrop,  with  the  peis  waist  •  lyand  on  the  eist  syide  of  the  clois,  with

to  the  lyne  stobbis,  with  the  eist  half  of  the  yairde,  payand  furtht  xvj  s. 
annuell  yeirle  for  relleif  of  ane  pairt  of  the  annuellis  of  the  said  place,  to 
stand  for  ane  pairt.  Secundle,  we  ordane  the  haile  west  syide,  begynand 
sevyn  inche  fra  the  foir  place,  but  ony  hurt  of  staris  or  wtheris  esiamentis,

50.  Elizabeth  Knox.        Eodem  die.     2  p.m.

upper  chamber,  and  wardrobe  (viz.,  a  forehouse  reserving  two  booths),  and  piece

thereto,  extending  to  "the  lyne  stob,"  together  with  the  east  half  of  the  yard; 
as  contained  in  the  decreet  of  division,  No.  49.  Thereafter  the  said  Elizabeth 
resigned  the  property  in  favor  of  herself  and  Alexander  Stewarde,  her 
husband.  Witnesses:  John  Stewarde  in  Bogtune,  John  Mertyne,  burgess, 
John  Seller,  David  Watsoun,  William  Hall,  Thomas  Andersoun,  William    Wat.

of  the  passage  or  way,  commonly  called  the  Wyndeheid,  to  the  church  of 
St.  Roche,  between  the  lands  of  the  hospital  of  St.  Nicolas,  founded  by  q.

on  account  of  the  forfeiture  of  the  said  Mathew;  and  which  place  the  Queen,

to    the    said    John    Hammyltoune    and    Elizabeth    Hammyltoune    as    heritable

57.  Stewards — Castelmylk.     2  October,   1550. 

David,  grandson  of  q.  Archinbalde  Stewarde  of  Cassiltoune,  produced  to 
John  Robertoune  of  Erknok,  sheriff  depute  of  Lanark,  a  precept  of  sasine, 
dated  4  July,  1550,  in  name  of  Mary,  Queen  of  Scots,  ordering  sasine  to 
be  given  to  the  said  David,  as  heir  of  his  grandfather,  of  the  lands  of 
Cassiltoune,  over  and  nether,  and  of  the  lands  of  Gallowhill,  with  tenants, 
tennandries,  and  free  tenants,  services  thereof,  and  all  and  sundry  pertinents, 
lying  in  the  barony  of  Curmannok  and  bailliary  of  Lanark;  saving  the  right  of 
every  one,  and  taking  security  for  £373  6s.  Sd.  of  the  ferms  of  the  said  lands, 
in  the  hands  of  the  sovereign,  as  in  right  of  the  earldom  of  Douglas  (tanquam 
comitis  de  Douglas),  for  the  space  of  6^  years,  on  account  of  ward,  and  for 
£53  6s.  8d.  owing  for  relief.  After  the  reading  of  the  precept,  the  sheriff  in 
that  part  gave  sasine  in  terms  thereof.  Done  on  the  ground  of  the  said  lands, 
at  the  principal  messuage  or  dwelling  place  thereof.  Present:  Alexander 
Stewarde,  son  of  Archinbald  Stewarde  in  Bowhows,  John  Stewarde,  Archinbald 
Kneland,  James  Gardner,  and  John  Forester.

59.  Bolde — Kilmarnock.     23  October,  1550. 

A  noble  and  potent  lord,  Robert,  master  of  Boyde,  produced  to  Robert 
Boyde,  younger  of  Portincors,  sheriff  or  bailie  in  that  j>art  specially  constituted, 
a  precept  of  sasine,  contained  in  a  charter  under  the  great  seal  granted  by

Hammyltoune,  protector  and  governor  of  the  kingdom,  dated  at  Jedburgh, 
14  October,  1550,1  charging  the  bailie  to  give  the  master  of  Boyde  sasine 
of  the  ten  pound  lands  of  old  extent  of  Auchintwerle,  Dunnerbuk  and  Spittell, 
and  mill  thereof,  fishings,  and  the  cobill  drawchtis  called  Crwkitschot  and 
Spitelschot,  upon  the  water  of  Clyde,  and  the  pertinents,  lying  within  the 
sheriffdom  of  Dunbertane;  which  lands  formerly  belonged  to  and  were  resigned 
by  John  lord  Lyile  with  consent  of  John  Lyndesay  of  Covingtoune.  After 
the  reading  of  the  precept,  the  sheriff  or  bailie  gave  sasine  of  the  lands  at 
the  principal  mesuage  thereof,  commonly  called  Auchintwerlie,  by  delivery  of 
earth  and  stone;  and  thereafter,  at  the  shore  of  the  water  of  Clyde,  and 
within  the  flow  of  the  sea,  gave  sasine  of  the  fishings  by  delivery  of  net 
and  coble.  Witnesses:  Patrick  Hammyltoune  of  Bogsyide,  James  Colquhoune, 
burgess  of  Glasgow,  Mr.  Adame  Colquhoune,  his  brother,  Alexander  Cwnyghame,

64.  Roger.  16  January,  1550-1. 

John  Mairjoribanks,  attorney,  and  in  name  of  John  Roger,  brother  and

tenement  on  the  west  side  of  the  said  street,  between  the  lands  of  q.  Thomas

81.  Gayne— Gayne.        11  May,   1551,  9  a.m. 

Jonet  Gayne,  daughter  and  heiress  of  q.  Eugenius  Gayne,  burgess,  produced 
to  Andrew  Dunlop,  bailie,  a  precept  of  sasine,  whereby  Mr.  John  Stewarde, 
canon  and  commissary-general  of  the  Metropolitan  Church  of  Glasgow,  conform 
to  inquest  made  by  his  authority,  and  retoured  to  his  chapel,  charged  the 
provost  and  bailies  of  the  city  of  Glasgow  to  give  the  said  Jonet  sasine  of 
a  tenement  of  land  lying  t.c.  in  the  street  which  passes  from  the  South  Port 
to  the  bridge  of  Clyde,  between  the  lands  of  q.  Thomas  Boid  on  the  west, 
and  the  lands  of  q.  John  Adam  on  the  east,  containing  in  length  five  ells; 
which  tenement  is  held  of  the  archbishop  of  Glasgow,  or  his  representative 
in  the  chapter,  for  payment  of  burgh  ferms.  The  tenement  was  then  valued 
at  five  merks  yearly,  and  in  time  of  peace  40  s.,  and  had  come  into  the 
hands  of  the  vicar-general  on  account  of  the  said  Jonet  not  prosecuting  her 
claim.  Sasine  given  by  the  bailie  in  terms  of  the  precept.  Witnesses: 
William  Leche,  in  Mekile  Govan,  senior;  James  Horner,  John  Stobo,  David 
Wylie,    notary,    and   John    Seller.

85.  Portkrfeilde — Stewarde — Calderuode.  27  May,  1551, 11  a.m.&  3  p.m. 
Robert  Maxuell,  attorney  of  Elizabeth  Stewarde,  dame  of  Calderuode,  produced 

to  William  Lyndsaye,  bailie  in  that  part,  a  precept  of  sasine  by  Mr.  John 
Porterfield  of  that  ilk,  charging  the  bailie  to  give  the  said  Elizabeth  sasine, 
in  liferent,  of  his  forty  shilling  lands  of  old  extent  of  Porterfeild,  with  the 
annexes,  connexes,  parts  and  pendicles  thereof,  lying  within  the  barony  of 
Ranfrow  and  sheriffdom  thereof ;  and  also  of  his  sixteen  shilling  lands  of 
Auchinquhorlie  within  the  same  barony  and  sheriffdom ;  all  conform  to 
a  charter  made  thereupon.  The  precept  was  signed  and  sealed  at  Glasgow,  15 
May,  1551,  in  presence  of  Robert  Maxuell,  Bartholomew  Alderstoun,  William 
Hegait,  and  John  Stewarde  of  Mynto.  After  the  reading  of  the  precept,  sasine 
was  given  in  terms  thereof,  first  on  the  grounds  of  Auchinquhorlie,  at  11  o'clock, 
in  presence  of  John  Lyoune,  senior,  John  Lyoune,  junior,  John  Tailyeour,  and 
Mathew  Bog,  and  afterwards  on  the  grounds  of  Porterfield,  at  3  o'clock,  in 
presence  of  Robert  Walkyngschaw  and  John  Patersoune.

103.  Cwnyghame — Hall,  Fowlbar.     29  November,  1551. 

In  presence  of  the  notary  and  witnesses,  appeared  David  Cwnyghame 
of  Robertland,  son  and  heir  of  q.  David  Cwnyghame  of  Robertland,  and 
represented  that  on  20  July,  1551,  lawful  premonition  had  been  made  to  him 
by  Arthur  Hall  of  Foulbar,  son  and  heir  of  q.  Jonet  Langmuir,  heir  portioner 
(to  the  extent  of  one  seventh)  of  q.  William  Langmuir  of  Auchinbothy,  to 
appear  personally  in  the  church  of  the  Friars  Preachers,  within  the  city  of 
Glasgow,  on  10  September  then  next,  between  the  rising  and  the  setting 
of  the  sun;  and  there,  upon  the  altar  of  B.V.  Mary,  to  receive  eighty  merks 
Scots  in  one  complete  sum,  together  with  a  sufficient  letter  of  tack,  for  the 
space  and  terms  of  six  years  immediately  following  the  said  10  September, 
of  all  and  whole  the  seventh  part  of  the  lands  of  Halkat  and  Hesilbankis, 
lying  within  the  bailliary  of  Cwnyghame  and  sheriffdom  of  Aire,  for  the 
payment  of  40  s.  Scots  yearly;  and  this  for  lawful  redemption  of  the  said 
seventh  part  of  Halkheid  and  Hesilbankis,  wadset  by  the  said  Jonet  to  the 
said  q.  David,  conform  to  letters  of  reversion  by  him,  dated  18  July,  1536, 
all  as  mentioned  in  the  instrument  of  premonition,  under  the  sign  and  sub- 
scription manual  of  Sir  Thomas  Raitht,  priest  and  notary  public.  The  said 
Arthur  Hall  appeared,  time  and  place  fixed,  and  laid  the  money  and  letter 
of  tack  upon  the  altar,  in  the  hands  of  John  Mwre,  bailie,  for  behoof  of 
the  said  David,  who  received  the  same  and  acknowledged  that  the  lands 
were    lawfully    redeemed,    and    renounced    all    right    and    title    thereto.      Done

137.  Isobella  Levingstoune.     29  August,  1552,  2  p.m. 

William  Gottray,  attorney  for  and  in  name  of  Isobella  Levyngstoune,  pro- 
duced a  precept  of  sasine,  whereby  Mr.  James  Levyngstoune  of  Baddorren 
charged  John  Grahame,  as  bailie  in  that  part,  to  give  sasine  to  the  said 
Isobella  Levyngstoune,  his  sister,  of  the  east  half  of  his  six  merk  lands  of  old 
extent  of  Garvok,  lying  within  the  sheriffdom  of  Renfrew  and  barony  thereof, 
and  this  in  consideration  of  a  certain  sum  of  money  paid  therefor.  The  precept 
is  signed  and  sealed  at  Glasgow,  29  March,  1552,  in  presence  of  John  Grahame 
of  Baddorren,  William  Levingstoune,  brother  german  of  the  granter,  John 
Grahame  of  Auchtincloicht,  William  Drew  and  James  Grahame,  burgess  of 
Glasgow.  After  the  reading  of  the  precept,  sasine  was  given  in  terms  thereof. 
Done  on  the  ground  of  the  lands  in  presence  of  Luke  Aitkyn,  Thomas  Aitkyn, 
and  Alexander  Walkar. 

138.  Hall,  Fowlbar.     1  September,  1552,  5  p.m. 

Arthur  Hall  of  Fowlbar  appeared  in  the  church  of  the  Friars  Preachers  of 
the  city  of  Glasgow,  and  there,  upon  the  altar  of  the  Blessed  Virgin,  produced  an 
instrument  of  premonition,  bearing  that  the  said  Arthur  Hall,  son  and  heir  of 
q.  Jonet  Langmuir,  one  of  the  heirs  portioners  of  the  lands  of  Auchinbothy, 
had  come  to  the  personal  presence  of  David  Cwnyghame  of  Robertlande,  in 
the  Metropolitan  Church  of  Glasgow,  in  the  aisle  of  St.  Michael  the  Archangel, 
and  warned  him  to  appear  in  the  church  of  the  Friars  Preachers,  at  the  said 
altar,  on  this  1st  of  September,  and  there  receive  the  sum  of  eighty  merks  for 
redemption  and  relief  of  the  seventh  part  of  the  lands  of  Watterlande  and 
mill  and  multures  thereof,  within  the  bailliary  of  Cwnyghame  and  sheriffdom  of 
Aire,  and  this  conform  to  letters  of  reversion  by  the  said  David  Cwnygham, 
son  and  heir  of  q.  David  Cwnyghame  of  Robertland,  to  the  said  Arthur,  heir  of 
said  Jonet,  dated  29  June,  1552.  The  said  David  appeared  and  received  the 
money,  and  renounced  all  right  to  the  lands  and  others.  Witnesses:  Mr.  William 
Hammyltoune,  rector  of  Cambuslang  and  official  general  of  Glasgow;  Mr.  John 
Hall,  Mr.  Robert  Herbisoune,  Thomas  Diksoune,  William  Swan,  and  William 
Fynny.

186.  Sir  Robert  Hill.     28  June,  1554,  1  p.m. 

Sir  Robert  Hill,  vicar  of  the  choir  of  the  Metropolitan  Church  of  Glasgow, 
produced  to  Andrew  Hammyltoune  of  Cochtnok,  bailie  of  the  city  of  Glasgow, 
a  precept  of  sasine  whereby  James,  Archbishop  of  Glasgow  (referring  to 
inquest  made  by  his  authority  and  retoured  to  his  chapel),  charged  Mr.  John 
Hall  and  John  Mwre,  bailies  of  the  burgh  and  city  of  Glasgow,  to  give 
sasine  to  him  as  heir  of  q.  Mr.  Robert  Hill  and  q.  Jonet  Hill,  sister  of  Mr. 
Robert,  of  an  annualrent  of  24  s.,  payable  furth  of  a  tenement,  with  yard 
and  pertinents,  lying  t.c.  in  the  street  leading  from  the  South  Port  to  the 
bridge  of  Clyde  on  the  north  side  thereof,  between  the  lands  or  tenement 
of  Allan  Morsoun  on  the  east,  and  the  lands  of  Ninian  Rois  on  the  west. 
The  precept  is  subscribed  by  the  Archbishop,  and  his  seal  appended,  at 
Edinburgh,  on  29  May,  1554,  in  presence  of  John  Mwre  and  Robert  Mershcell, 
chaplains;  William  Fogo,  the  archbishop's  secretary;  and  Mr.  James  Thornetoun, 
canon  of  his  church  of  Glasgow.  After  the  reading  of  the  precept,  the  bailie 
gave  sasine  in  terms  thereof.  Witnesses:  Robert  Gilmour,  John  Patersoune, 
and   James   Dalgleis.

John  Mwre,  bailie,  gave  sasine  to  Mathew  Wilsoune,  as  heir  of  Archinbald 

Wilsoune,  his  father,  of  (1)  a  tenement,  with  yard  and  pertinents,  lying  t.c. 
in  the  great  street  leading  from  the  Metropolitan  Church  to  the  Market 
Cross,  on  the  west  side  thereof,  between  the  lands  of  q.  James  Fari  on  the 
south,  the  lands  sometime  of  Robert  Baxter,  now  of  Mr.  Robert  Herbisoune, 
on  the  north,  and  the  street  on  the  east.  (2)  Another  tenement  in  the 
same  street,  on  the  east  side  thereof,  between  the  lands  or  tenement  of  Sir 
Peter  Law  on  the  south,  the  lands  or  tenement  of  the  Collegiate  Church 
of  B.V.  Mary  on  the  north,  and  the  back  tenement  of  q.  John  Herbisoune 
on  the  east.  (3)  A  barn  lying  in  ly  Stokucll,  between  the  lands  of  q.  John 
Govane  on  the  south,  the  barn  or  lands  of  Mr.  John  Hall  on  the  north, 
the  street  on  the  west,  and  the  lands  of  Mwteland  Croft  on  the  east. 
Witnesses:  Robert  Gilmour,  Cuthbert  Gilmour,  John  Wilsoun,  and  David 
Watsoun,  Serjeant.

father,  of  a  waste  barn,  with  two  rigs  of  land  adjacent  thereto,  lying  in 
ly  Dofjliillok,  within  the  bounds  of  the  foresaid  sub-dean,  having  his  special  bailie 
from  of  old.     Same  witnesses  as  in  preceding  protocol.

the  pertinents,  lying  in  ly  Stokucll,  having  a  yard  adjoining,  between  the 
lands   of   q.    John    Govane   on    the   south,    the    lands   of    Mr.    John    or   Arthur

Hall   on   the   north,  the   lands  of   Mwtelande  Croft  on   the  east,  and  the  street

213.  Robert  Heriot.     9  March,  1554-5,  2  p.m. 

John  Mwre,  bailie,  gave  sasine  to  Robert  Heriot  of  two  roods  of  moor 
land  of  the  Gallow  Mure,  with  the  pendicles  thereof  as  measured,  granted  to 
him  in  feu  farm  by  the  community  and  city  of  Glasgow,  conform  to  a  feu 
charter,  for  payment  of  an  annualrent  of  5  s.  Witnesses:  Robert  Mwre, 
John  Boide,  William  Wat,  James  Mayne,  Alexander  Stene,  Bartholomew 
Alderstone,  comburgesses  of  Glasgow;  David  Watsoune  and  Robert  Burne, 
Serjeants. 

214.  Mr.  John  Hall.  Eodem  die  et  hora.

community,  lying  in  ly  Gallow  Mwre,  between  other  two  roods  conveyed  to 
John  Wilsoune  on  the  east,  and  the  commonty  or  common  on  the  west; 
for   payment   of   an   annualrent   of   5  s.    6  d.     Same   witnesses. 

215.  John  Wilsoune.        Eodem  die  et  hora.

or  roods  conveyed  to  Robert  Heriot  on  the  east,  and  the  lands  or  roods 
conveyed  to  Mr.  John  Hall  on  the  west,  conform  to  a  charter  granted  by 
the  bailies,  council,  and  community;  for  payment  of  an  annualrent  of  6  s. 
Same   witnesses. 

216.  Patrick  Hammyltoune  and  his  Spouse.    14  March,  1554-5,  10  a.m. 

Patrick  Hammyltoune  of  Bogsyide  produced  to  John  Smyth,  bailie  in 
that  part,  a  precept  of  sasine  granted  by  John,  Archbishop  of  St.  Andrews, 
primate  of  the  kingdom  of  Scotland,  and  also  perpetual  commendator  of 
the  monastery  of  Paisley,  and  Mr.  Thomas  Marjorybankis  of  Ratko,  com- 
missioners and  procurators  general  of  lord  James  Hammyltoune,  lord  fiar  of 
the   earldom   of   Arran   and   lordship   of    Hammyltoune,    having  general   power

from  him  to  enter  free  tenants  to  the  said  earldom  and  lordship,  during  his 
absence  in  France;  by  which  precept  the  said  commissioners,  with  consent 
of  lord  James,  duke  of  Chattewlaraut,  lord  liferenter  of  the  earldom  and 
lordship,  charged  the  bailie  to  give  sasine  to  the  said  Patrick  Hammyltoune 
of  Bogsyide,  brother  german  of  q.  James  Hammyltoune  of  Schawfeilde,  and 
Joanna  Boyde,  his  spouse,  and  1.1.,  and  their  heirs,  whom  failing  to  Elizabetht 
Hammyltoune,  daughter  of  the  said  q.  James  Hammyltoune  of  Schawfeilde, 
and  her  heirs,  sasine  of  the  lands  of  Newtoune  of  Cambuslainge  and  the 
lands  of  Mwrganeholme,  with  the  pertinents,  lying  in  the  barony  of  Drwmsargat 
and  sheriffdom  of  Lanark,  holding  of  the  said  lord  James,  lord  fiar  of  the 
said  earldom  and  lordship,  and  also  of  the  said  duke  of  Chatteralaut,  lord 
liferenter  thereof,  for  his  interest,  and  of  their  successors,  barons  of  the 
barony  of  Drwmsargat,  as  contained  in  a  charter  referred  to  in  the  precept. 
The  precept  is  signed  and  sealed  at  Edinburgh,  7  March,  1554,  in  presence 
of  Mr.  Alexander  Fores,  provost  of  Mary  in  the  Fields  (Marie  de  CampisJ; 
William  Watsoune,  Archinbald  Hammyltoune,  and  James  Hammyltoune  of 
Ormystoune.  Sasine  given  to  Patrick  Hammyltoune  and  spouse,  at  the 
principal  messuage  of  the  lands.  Witnesses:  David  Lyndesay  of  Kittoksyide, 
Mr.  Andrew  Laing,  rector  of  Hoddome;  Robert  Miller  in  Clydismylne,  William 
Mader  in  Newtoune  of  Cambuslainge,  William  Lop  there,  William  Watsoune 
and   John    Hammyltoune  in   Schawtoune.

John  Herbisoune,  son  of  q.  John  Herbisoune,  burgess  of  Glesgw,  resigned 
the  property  mentioned  in  No.  231,  in  favor  of  John  Boge,  to  be  enjoyed 
by  him  till  he  received  the  sum  of  £40,  which  the  granter  (John  Herbisoune) 
had  promised  him  as  dowry  with  his  sister,  but  which  sum  he  did  not  then 
possess.  Sasine  given  by  John  Mwre,  bailie.  Witnesses:  John  Sym,  David 
Armoure,  Andrew  Arneile,  James  Dalgleis,  and  Robert  Burne,  serjeant. 

233.  Andrew  Creichtoune.     2  August,  1555,  9  a.m. 

Robert  Greneleis,  attorney,  and  in  name  of  Andrew  Creichtoune,  son  of  q. 
Robert  lord  Creichtoune,  produced  a  precept  by  Robert  lord  Sempile,  charging 
his  bailies  to  give  to  Andrew  Creichtoune  sasine  of  the  liferent  of  the  granter's 
£5  land  of  old  extent  of  Chittirflatis,  now  valued  at  100  merks  per  annum, 
lying  in  the  barony  of  Renfrew  and  sheriffdom  thereof,  holding  of  our  Sovereign 
Lady  the  Queen,  as  contained  in  a  charter  made  thereupon.  The  precept  is

246.  Patrick  Howstoune.     6  November,  1555,  between  3  and  4  p.m. 

In  the  church  of  St.  Giles  of  Edinburgh,  at  the  altar  of  St.  James  the 
Apostle,  Patrick  Howstoune,  of  that  ilk,  appeared  in  presence  of  Francis 
Tenende,  burgess  of  the  said  burgh,  in  terms  of  lawful  premonition,  to  receive 
the  sums  contained  in  four  letters  of  reversion,  together  with  three  letters  of 
assedation,  for  redemption  of  the  lands  of  Lany  and  Aldirstoune,  lying  within 
the  barony  of  Calder  and  sheriffdom  of  Edinburgh,  according  to  the  tenor  of  the 
said  reversions  granted  by  q.  Kenthigern  Tenende.  The  sum  contained  in  the 
letters  of  reversion,  amounting  to  1010  merks  Scots,  having  been  laid  upon  the 
altar,  in  gold  and  silver,  the  said  Patrick  produced  the  said  four  letters  of 
reversion,  with  the  three  letters  of  assedation — two  whereof  of  the  fourth  part  of 
the  lands  of  Lany  and  the  third  of  the  half  of  the  lands  of  Upper  Aldirstoune, 
sealed  and  subscribed  by  the  said  Patrick,  together  with  an  assignation  therein 
contained,  to  Agnes  Hoppringeile,  his  mother,  constituting  her  asssignee  in  and 
to  two  letters  of  reversion  of  the  lands  of  Lany;  and  also  produced  the  instru- 
ment of  premonition  whereby  the  said  Francis,  as  principal,  together  with  George 
Dundas,  son  of  q.  James  Dundas  of  that  ilk,  Mergaret  Dundas,  daughter  of 
the  said  q.  James,  heritable  possessors  of  one  part  of  the  said  lands  of  Lany, 
and  Mergaret  Sandeland,  mother  of  the"  said  Mergaret,  were  warned  to  compear 
at  the  said  altar,  there  to  receive  the  said  sums  and  letters  of  assedation  for 
implement  of  the  said  letters  of  reversion,  and  for  redemption  and  renunciation 
of  the  said  lands.  And  because .  neither  the  said  Francis,  nor  any  other  in  his 
name  was  willing  to  receive  the  money  and  letters  and  free  the  lands,  the  said 
Patrick  came  to  the  personal  presence  of  Archibald  Dowglas,  provost  of  Edin- 
burght,  James  Barroune,  dean  of  guild,  Mr.  Alexander  Grahame,  treasurer  of  the 
said  burgh,  and  required  them  to  receive  into  their  custody  the  said  sums,  with 
the  said  letters  of  assedation,  for  behoof  of  the  said  Francis  and  others  having 
interest.  And  the  said  Patrick  consigned  in  the  hands  of  Mr.  John  Prestoune 
the  sum  of  345  merks,  with  a  letter  of  assedation  for  redemption  of  the  half  of 
a  fourth  part  of  the  lands  of  Lany,  and  consigned  the  remainder  of  the  said  sum, 
with  the  other  two  letters  of  assedation,  in  the  hands  of  David  Kynloicht,  baker,

and  burgess  of  the  said  burgh,  for  redemption  of  the  other  half  of  the  fourth

247.  John  Morisoune,  Burgess  of  Edinburgh.    9  November,  1555,  3.  p.m.

subscription  manual  of  Mr.  John  Rais,  notary  public,  dated  28th  September, 
1554;  and  he  also  produced  instrument  of  premonition,  requiring  the  said 
Robert  to  compear  this  day  at  the  said  altar,  and  receive  the  said  sums 
and  letters  of  assedation;  and  straightway  laid  upon  the  altar  the  said  two 
letters  of  reversion,  two  letters  of  assedation,  and  the  foresaid  sums  of  money 
in  gold  and  silver,  which  the  said  Robert  Nasmyth  received,  and  he  renounced 
all    claim   to   the   tenement  and    lands,  saving  his   rights   under  the  said   letters
